Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Critical

Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender

Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] SnakeStealer SMTP Credential Exfiltration to Public Webmail Relays from Non-Mail Client · [LLM] SnakeStealer Wi-Fi Credential Harvest via netsh wlan show profile key=clear · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
[LLM] SnakeStealer Telegram Bot Exfiltration via api.telegram.org from Non-Telegram Process Command & Control High
Detects non-Telegram-Desktop processes initiating outbound traffic or DNS to api.telegram.org — the bot-API endpoint Snake Keylogger/SnakeStealer uses (POST to /bot<TOKEN>/sendMessage|sendDocument) to ship harvested credentials, keystrokes and screenshots. Article-specific because the body explicitly names Telegram bots as an exfiltration channel. Rationale: Article explicitly states SnakeStealer 'sends stolen data via FTP, HTTP, email, or Telegram bots.' Splunk, Fortinet and ANY.RUN analyses corroborate that the exact shape is HTTP POST to api.telegram.org/bot<token>/sendMessage|sendDocument — high-fidelity when the initiating process is NOT the Telegram desktop client (which is the only legit user of that host in most enterprises). Cross-checked against: • https://www.splunk.com/en_us/blog/security/under-the-hood-of-snakekeylogger-analyzing-its-loader-and-its-tactics-techniques-and-procedures.html • https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant • https://any.run/cybersecurity-blog/analyzing-snake-keylogger/ • https://hackread.com/snake-keylogger-variant-windows-data-telegram-bots/
ATT&CKT1102.002 T1567.003
Data sourcesWeb.Web Network_Resolution.DNS
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteUrl has "api.telegram.org"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("telegram.exe","updater.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !contains @"\Telegram Desktop\"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName,
          AccountUpn=InitiatingProcessAccountUpn,
          ProcessName=InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ProcessPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ProcessCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ProcessSHA256=InitiatingProcessSHA256,
          RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
let TelegramQueries =
    DnsEvents
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where Name endswith "api.telegram.org"
    | project DnsTime=TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name;
let ProcessContext =
    SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where NewProcessName !endswith @"\Telegram.exe"
    | where NewProcessName !endswith @"\Updater.exe"
    | project ProcessTime=TimeGenerated, Computer, NewProcessName, CommandLine, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName;
TelegramQueries
| join kind=leftouter ProcessContext on Computer
| where isempty(ProcessTime) or abs(datetime_diff('second', DnsTime, ProcessTime)) <= 120
| project DnsTime, ProcessTime, Computer, ClientIP, Name, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by DnsTime desc
title: SnakeStealer Telegram Bot API Beacon
id: e8a3f9c1-7b22-4d45-9d11-2c5e7a8b3f01
description: Detects DNS resolution of api.telegram.org from non-Telegram-Desktop processes, a Snake Keylogger / SnakeStealer C2 and exfiltration channel.
references:
  - https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/malware/snakestealer-personal-data-stay-safe/
  - https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant
  - https://any.run/cybersecurity-blog/analyzing-snake-keylogger/
author: SOC Detection Eng
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1102.002
  - attack.t1567.003
  - attack.command_and_control
  - attack.exfiltration
logsource:
  category: dns
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    QueryName|endswith: 'api.telegram.org'
  filter_telegram_client:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\Telegram.exe'
      - '\TelegramDesktop.exe'
      - '\Updater.exe'
  condition: selection and not filter_telegram_client
falsepositives:
  - Legitimate Telegram Desktop client
  - Developer running a personal Telegram bot from python.exe
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:22 @QueryName:api.telegram.org -@Image:(*\\Telegram.exe OR *\\telegram.exe OR *\\TelegramDesktop.exe OR *\\Updater.exe OR *\\TELEGRAM.EXE)
#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/api\.telegram\.org/i
| ContextImageFileName!=/\\(Telegram|TelegramDesktop|Updater)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, DomainName], function=count())
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Web.url) as url values(Web.http_method) as method values(Web.dest) as dest from datamodel=Web where Web.url="*api.telegram.org/bot*" AND NOT Web.process IN ("telegram.exe","Telegram.exe","TelegramDesktop.exe","Updater.exe") by Web.src Web.user Web.process Web.dest | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | rename firstTime as _time | convert ctime(_time) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Web.url) as url values(Web.http_method) as method values(Web.dest) as dest from datamodel=Web where Web.url="*api.telegram.org/bot*" AND NOT Web.process IN ("telegram.exe","Telegram.exe","TelegramDesktop.exe","Updater.exe") by Web.src Web.user Web.process Web.dest | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | rename firstTime as _time | convert ctime(_time) ctime(lastTime)
[LLM] SnakeStealer SMTP Credential Exfiltration to Public Webmail Relays from Non-Mail Client Actions on Objectives High
Snake Keylogger ships stolen credentials over SMTP submission (port 587/465/25) to attacker-controlled mailboxes on public webmail relays — smtp.zoho.com, smtp.gmail.com, smtp.mail.ru, smtp.yandex.com, etc. Detects direct SMTP connections from any process that isn't a known mail client. Article-specific because the body lists email as a primary exfil channel. Rationale: Article lists 'email' as one of SnakeStealer's four exfiltration channels. Fortinet, Splunk and ANY.RUN explicitly observed Snake variants connecting to smtp.zoho.com:587 (recipients inquirysmtp@zohomail.com, recovery@tmcksa.com) and other public webmail SMTP relays to mail the stolen-credentials report. Submission-port traffic from a non-mail-client process is high-signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.splunk.com/en_us/blog/security/under-the-hood-of-snakekeylogger-analyzing-its-loader-and-its-tactics-techniques-and-procedures.html • https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant • https://any.run/cybersecurity-blog/analyzing-snake-keylogger/
ATT&CKT1048.003 T1071.003
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
let SmtpHosts = dynamic(["smtp.zoho.com","smtp.gmail.com","smtp-mail.outlook.com","smtp.mail.yahoo.com","smtp.yandex.com","smtp.mail.ru","mail.privateemail.com"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemotePort in (587, 465, 25)
| where RemoteUrl has_any (SmtpHosts)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("outlook.exe","thunderbird.exe","emclient.exe","mailbird.exe","msimn.exe","hostedoffice.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !contains @"\Microsoft Office\"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName,
          AccountUpn=InitiatingProcessAccountUpn,
          ProcessName=InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ProcessPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ProcessCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ProcessSHA256=InitiatingProcessSHA256,
          RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
let SmtpHosts = dynamic(["smtp.zoho.com","smtp.gmail.com","smtp-mail.outlook.com","smtp.mail.yahoo.com","smtp.yandex.com","smtp.mail.ru","mail.privateemail.com"]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstPortNumber in (587, 465, 25)
| where DstHostname has_any (SmtpHosts)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, NetworkBytes
| join kind=leftouter (
    SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where NewProcessName !endswith @"\outlook.exe"
    | where NewProcessName !endswith @"\thunderbird.exe"
    | where NewProcessName !endswith @"\emclient.exe"
    | where NewProcessName !endswith @"\mailbird.exe"
    | project ProcessTime=TimeGenerated, Computer, NewProcessName, CommandLine, SubjectUserName
  ) on $left.DvcHostname == $right.Computer
| where isempty(ProcessTime) or abs(datetime_diff('second', TimeGenerated, ProcessTime)) <= 120
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: SnakeStealer SMTP Credential Exfiltration from Non-Mail Client
id: 5c4b1a9e-3f78-4a22-bd66-91a2e7c4d3f2
description: Detects SMTP submission (587/465/25) to public webmail SMTP relays from processes that are not known mail clients, consistent with Snake Keylogger / SnakeStealer mailing harvested credentials to attacker-controlled mailboxes.
references:
  - https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/malware/snakestealer-personal-data-stay-safe/
  - https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant
  - https://any.run/cybersecurity-blog/analyzing-snake-keylogger/
author: SOC Detection Eng
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1048.003
  - attack.t1071.003
  - attack.exfiltration
logsource:
  category: network_connection
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    DestinationPort:
      - 587
      - 465
      - 25
    DestinationHostname|contains:
      - 'smtp.zoho.com'
      - 'smtp.gmail.com'
      - 'smtp-mail.outlook.com'
      - 'smtp.mail.yahoo.com'
      - 'smtp.yandex.com'
      - 'smtp.mail.ru'
      - 'mail.privateemail.com'
  filter_mail_clients:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\outlook.exe'
      - '\thunderbird.exe'
      - '\emclient.exe'
      - '\mailbird.exe'
      - '\msimn.exe'
  condition: selection and not filter_mail_clients
falsepositives:
  - Corporate Send-MailMessage scheduled tasks relaying through Gmail/Zoho
  - Backup/monitoring agents emailing reports via public SMTP
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 @DestinationPort:(587 OR 465 OR 25) @DestinationHostname:(smtp.zoho.com OR smtp.gmail.com OR smtp-mail.outlook.com OR smtp.mail.yahoo.com OR smtp.yandex.com OR smtp.mail.ru OR mail.privateemail.com) -@Image:(*\\outlook.exe OR *\\OUTLOOK.EXE OR *\\Outlook.exe OR *\\thunderbird.exe OR *\\Thunderbird.exe OR *\\emclient.exe OR *\\mailbird.exe OR *\\msimn.exe)
#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/^(smtp\.(zoho|gmail|yandex|mail\.ru)\.com|smtp-mail\.outlook\.com|smtp\.mail\.yahoo\.com|mail\.privateemail\.com)$/i
| ContextImageFileName!=/\\(outlook|thunderbird|emclient|mailbird|msimn)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, DomainName], function=count())

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(All_Traffic.dest) as dest values(All_Traffic.process) as process values(All_Traffic.user) as user from datamodel=Network_Traffic where All_Traffic.dest_port IN (587,465,25) AND All_Traffic.dest IN ("smtp.zoho.com","smtp.gmail.com","smtp-mail.outlook.com","smtp.mail.yahoo.com","smtp.yandex.com","smtp.mail.ru","mail.privateemail.com") AND NOT All_Traffic.process IN ("outlook.exe","thunderbird.exe","emclient.exe","mailbird.exe","msimn.exe","HostedOffice.exe") by All_Traffic.src All_Traffic.dest All_Traffic.dest_port All_Traffic.process | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)` | rename firstTime as _time | convert ctime(_time) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(All_Traffic.dest) as dest values(All_Traffic.process) as process values(All_Traffic.user) as user from datamodel=Network_Traffic where All_Traffic.dest_port IN (587,465,25) AND All_Traffic.dest IN ("smtp.zoho.com","smtp.gmail.com","smtp-mail.outlook.com","smtp.mail.yahoo.com","smtp.yandex.com","smtp.mail.ru","mail.privateemail.com") AND NOT All_Traffic.process IN ("outlook.exe","thunderbird.exe","emclient.exe","mailbird.exe","msimn.exe","HostedOffice.exe") by All_Traffic.src All_Traffic.dest All_Traffic.dest_port All_Traffic.process | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)` | rename firstTime as _time | convert ctime(_time) ctime(lastTime)
[LLM] SnakeStealer Startup-Folder Persistence (ageless.vbs / .exe drop in Programs\Startup) Installation High
Snake Keylogger achieves logon persistence by dropping a small VBS launcher (HP Wolf observed ageless.vbs calling WScript.Shell.Run on ageless.exe) into the per-user Startup folder. Detects non-installer processes writing .vbs / .js / .lnk / .exe / .bat / .wsf / .hta into %AppData%\Roaming\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup\. Article-specific because the body states SnakeStealer 'alters Windows boot configurations to maintain access.' Rationale: Article states SnakeStealer 'alters Windows boot configurations to maintain access on compromised systems.' HP Wolf and Fortinet research specifically document the per-user Startup folder drop (e.g. ageless.vbs → ageless.exe). Targeting the file-event shape — non-installer process writing an executable launcher into Programs\Startup — catches the actual TTP irrespective of the specific filename variant Snake operators pick. Cross-checked against: • https://threatresearch.ext.hp.com/the-many-skins-of-snake-keylogger/ • https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1547/001/
ATT&CKT1547.001 T1059.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has @"\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup\"
| where FileName endswith ".vbs" or FileName endswith ".js"
       or FileName endswith ".lnk" or FileName endswith ".exe"
       or FileName endswith ".bat" or FileName endswith ".cmd"
       or FileName endswith ".wsf" or FileName endswith ".hta"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("explorer.exe","msiexec.exe","setup.exe","TrustedInstaller.exe","OfficeClickToRun.exe","OneDriveSetup.exe","Update.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Program Files"
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\System32\"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName,
          AccountUpn=InitiatingProcessAccountUpn,
          FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          DropperFile=InitiatingProcessFileName,
          DropperPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          DropperCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          DropperSHA256=InitiatingProcessSHA256,
          DropperCompany=InitiatingProcessVersionInfoCompanyName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where TargetFilePath has @"\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup\"
| where TargetFileExtension in~ ("vbs","js","lnk","exe","bat","cmd","wsf","hta")
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("explorer.exe","msiexec.exe","setup.exe","TrustedInstaller.exe","OfficeClickToRun.exe","OneDriveSetup.exe")
| where ActingProcessName !startswith @"C:\Program Files"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: SnakeStealer Startup-Folder Persistence (ageless.vbs pattern)
id: d29c4f81-1e57-4a82-9c33-7b8d2f1e6a09
description: Detects non-installer processes writing executable launchers (.vbs / .js / .lnk / .exe / .bat / .wsf / .hta) into the per-user Startup folder — the persistence shape used by Snake Keylogger / SnakeStealer (HP Wolf research observed ageless.vbs dropping ageless.exe).
references:
  - https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/malware/snakestealer-personal-data-stay-safe/
  - https://threatresearch.ext.hp.com/the-many-skins-of-snake-keylogger/
  - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1547/001/
author: SOC Detection Eng
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1547.001
  - attack.t1059.005
  - attack.persistence
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    TargetFilename|contains: '\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup\'
    TargetFilename|endswith:
      - '.vbs'
      - '.js'
      - '.lnk'
      - '.exe'
      - '.bat'
      - '.cmd'
      - '.wsf'
      - '.hta'
  filter_legit_installers:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\explorer.exe'
      - '\msiexec.exe'
      - '\setup.exe'
      - '\TrustedInstaller.exe'
      - '\OfficeClickToRun.exe'
      - '\OneDriveSetup.exe'
      - '\Update.exe'
  condition: selection and not filter_legit_installers
falsepositives:
  - Application installers placing autostart shortcuts (OneDrive, Slack, Spotify)
  - SCCM/Intune-pushed startup items
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @TargetFilename:*Startup* @TargetFilename:(*.vbs OR *.js OR *.lnk OR *.exe OR *.bat OR *.cmd OR *.wsf OR *.hta) -@Image:(*\\explorer.exe OR *\\Explorer.exe OR *\\msiexec.exe OR *\\setup.exe OR *\\Setup.exe OR *\\TrustedInstaller.exe OR *\\OfficeClickToRun.exe OR *\\OneDriveSetup.exe)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten TargetFileName=/\\Microsoft\\Windows\\Start Menu\\Programs\\Startup\\.+\.(vbs|js|lnk|exe|bat|cmd|wsf|hta)$/i
| ContextImageFileName!=/\\(explorer|msiexec|setup|TrustedInstaller|OfficeClickToRun|OneDriveSetup|Update)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Filesystem.user) as user values(Filesystem.process_name) as process values(Filesystem.file_hash) as file_hash from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.action=created AND Filesystem.file_path="*\\Microsoft\\Windows\\Start Menu\\Programs\\Startup\\*" AND (Filesystem.file_name="*.vbs" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.js" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.lnk" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.exe" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.bat" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.cmd" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.wsf" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.hta") AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("explorer.exe","setup.exe","msiexec.exe","TrustedInstaller.exe","OfficeClickToRun.exe","OneDriveSetup.exe") by host Filesystem.user Filesystem.file_name Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | rename firstTime as _time | convert ctime(_time) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Filesystem.user) as user values(Filesystem.process_name) as process values(Filesystem.file_hash) as file_hash from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.action=created AND Filesystem.file_path="*\\Microsoft\\Windows\\Start Menu\\Programs\\Startup\\*" AND (Filesystem.file_name="*.vbs" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.js" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.lnk" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.exe" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.bat" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.cmd" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.wsf" OR Filesystem.file_name="*.hta") AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("explorer.exe","setup.exe","msiexec.exe","TrustedInstaller.exe","OfficeClickToRun.exe","OneDriveSetup.exe") by host Filesystem.user Filesystem.file_name Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | rename firstTime as _time | convert ctime(_time) ctime(lastTime)
[LLM] SnakeStealer Wi-Fi Credential Harvest via netsh wlan show profile key=clear Actions on Objectives High
SnakeStealer enumerates saved Wi-Fi PSKs by invoking netsh.exe wlan show profile name=<SSID> key=clear and shipping the plaintext keys alongside browser/Discord credentials. Detects netsh.exe with the key=clear option spawned by a non-admin-console parent — especially when the parent lives in %AppData% / %Temp%. Article-specific because the body names Wi-Fi networks as a credential-theft target. Rationale: Article explicitly states SnakeStealer 'extracts saved passwords from web browsers, databases, email and chat clients, including Discord, and Wi-Fi networks.' Fortinet, ANY.RUN and HP Wolf analyses document the exact shell shape: `netsh wlan show profile name="<SSID>" key=clear` invoked per-SSID by the .NET stealer payload. `key=clear` is the canonical credential-dump option — normal admins rarely type it. Cross-checked against: • https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant • https://any.run/cybersecurity-blog/analyzing-snake-keylogger/ • https://threatresearch.ext.hp.com/the-many-skins-of-snake-keylogger/
ATT&CKT1555 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName =~ "netsh.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "wlan"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "key=clear" or ProcessCommandLine has "key = clear"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("mmc.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe","SCNotification.exe")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| extend ParentInUserWriteable = (InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\AppData\Local\Temp\"
                                  or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\AppData\Roaming\"
                                  or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\Users\Public\"
                                  or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\ProgramData\")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, AccountUpn,
          ChildCmd=ProcessCommandLine,
          ParentName=InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ParentSHA256=InitiatingProcessSHA256,
          ParentCompany=InitiatingProcessVersionInfoCompanyName,
          ParentInUserWriteable
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith @"\netsh.exe"
| where CommandLine has "wlan"
| where CommandLine has "key=clear" or CommandLine has "key = clear"
| where SubjectUserName !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName !endswith @"\mmc.exe"
| where ParentProcessName !endswith @"\wininit.exe"
| where ParentProcessName !endswith @"\services.exe"
| extend ParentInUserWriteable = (ParentProcessName has @"\AppData\Local\Temp\"
                                  or ParentProcessName has @"\AppData\Roaming\"
                                  or ParentProcessName has @"\Users\Public\"
                                  or ParentProcessName has @"\ProgramData\")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentInUserWriteable
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: SnakeStealer Wi-Fi Credential Harvest via netsh wlan key=clear
id: 6f3b8e21-9c47-4a58-bc91-3e1d7f2a5b40
description: Detects netsh.exe enumerating saved Wi-Fi profiles with `key=clear` (dumping plaintext PSKs to stdout) — a Snake Keylogger / SnakeStealer credential-access TTP for the Wi-Fi keys the malware exfiltrates alongside browser passwords.
references:
  - https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/malware/snakestealer-personal-data-stay-safe/
  - https://www.fortinet.com/blog/threat-research/deep-analysis-of-snake-keylogger-new-variant
  - https://any.run/cybersecurity-blog/analyzing-snake-keylogger/
author: SOC Detection Eng
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1555
  - attack.t1059.003
  - attack.credential_access
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_img:
    Image|endswith: '\netsh.exe'
  selection_cmd:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'wlan'
    CommandLine|contains|all:
      - 'key=clear'
  filter_admin_console:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\mmc.exe'
      - '\wininit.exe'
      - '\services.exe'

falsepositives:
  - Help-desk technician manually pulling Wi-Fi PSKs during user troubleshooting
  - IT inventory or laptop-migration script
  - Penetration test / red-team simulation
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @Image:(*\\netsh.exe OR *\\NETSH.EXE OR *\\Netsh.exe) @CommandLine:*wlan* @CommandLine:*key=clear* -@ParentImage:(*\\mmc.exe OR *\\wininit.exe OR *\\services.exe OR *\\SCNotification.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\netsh\.exe$/i CommandLine=/wlan/i CommandLine=/key\s*=\s*clear/i
| ParentImageFileName!=/\\(mmc|wininit|services|SCNotification)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'

        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)

              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;

// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;

   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children

description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when

references:

            - '\mshta.exe'

            - '\wmic.exe'

            - '\Program Files (x86)\'

title: Remote-Management (RMM) Tool Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0c
status: experimental
description: |
    AnyDesk / TeamViewer / ScreenConnect / Atera / Splashtop /
    RustDesk — RMM tools attackers commonly install for hands-on-
    keyboard access. Filter against the IT estate's known tool.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1219/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1219
    - attack.command-and-control
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\AnyDesk.exe'
            - '\TeamViewer.exe'
            - '\TeamViewer_Service.exe'
            - '\ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe'
            - '\ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe'
            - '\atera_agent.exe'
            - '\SplashtopStreamer.exe'
            - '\RustDesk.exe'
            - '\NinjaOne.exe'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - IT-approved RMM in use across the estate. Maintain a known-IT-
      tool exclusion list per environment.
level: high
ENDPOINT

AI-aided malvertising: Exploiting a chatbot to spread scams

ESET WeLiveSecurity
2025-10-13 6 use cases 4 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

Cybercriminals have tricked X’s AI chatbot into promoting phishing scams in a technique that has been nicknamed “Grokking”. Here’s what to know about it.

ATT&CKT1190 T1566 T1566.001 T1566.002
Domainsdatingprudethimble.com
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
—
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Likely
Phase 6

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Connection to Grok malvertising domain (datingprudethimble.com) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.

Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
[LLM] Connection to Grok malvertising domain (datingprudethimble.com) Delivery High
Detects DNS resolution, network connection, or URL click to datingprudethimble.com — the credential-stealing/malware domain identified in the October 2025 X/Grok malvertising campaign where threat actors abused indirect prompt injection on Grok video-card posts to amplify the link to millions of impressions. Any endpoint hit indicates a likely victim who followed a Grok-amplified scam link. Rationale: Domain datingprudethimble.com is the only concrete IOC in the article (pre-extracted) and was named as one of the credential-stealing landing pages amplified by Grok in the X malvertising campaign. A connection from any internal host to this domain is high-fidelity evidence the user fell for the Grok-amplified scam link — domain has no legitimate business use case. Cross-channel coverage (DNS + URL click + network + proxy) catches the visit even when the browser uses DoH or the click telemetry is missing. Cross-checked against: • https://x.com/bananahacks/status/1963184353250353488
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1071.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web Network_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
let BadDomain = "datingprudethimble.com";
union isfuzzy=true
  (DeviceNetworkEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where RemoteUrl has BadDomain
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Src="DeviceNetworkEvents"),
  (UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where Url has BadDomain
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName="", AccountName=AccountUpn, RemoteUrl=Url, RemoteIP=IPAddress, RemotePort=int(null), InitiatingProcessFileName=Workload, InitiatingProcessCommandLine=tostring(ActionType), Src="UrlClickEvents"),
  (DeviceEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ActionType in ("BrowserLaunchedToOpenUrl","ConnectionFound") and RemoteUrl has BadDomain
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Src="DeviceEvents")
| order by Timestamp desc
let BadDomain = "datingprudethimble.com";
union isfuzzy=true
  (DnsEvents
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where Name has BadDomain or Question has BadDomain
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, IPAddresses=Result, Src="DnsEvents"),
  (CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where DestinationHostName has BadDomain or RequestURL has BadDomain
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer=SourceHostName, ClientIP=SourceIP, Name=DestinationHostName, QueryType=RequestMethod, IPAddresses=DestinationIP, Src="CommonSecurityLog"),
  (ImDnsActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where DnsQuery has BadDomain
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer=DvcHostname, ClientIP=SrcIpAddr, Name=DnsQuery, QueryType=DnsQueryTypeName, IPAddresses=DstHostname, Src="ImDnsActivity"),
  (ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where Url has BadDomain or HttpHostName has BadDomain
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer=DvcHostname, ClientIP=SrcIpAddr, Name=HttpHostName, QueryType=HttpMethod, IPAddresses=DstIpAddr, Src="ImWebSession")
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Network or DNS Connection to Grok Malvertising Domain (datingprudethimble.com)
id: a7c8d4e2-1b3f-4a9d-8e6c-5f7b2a9c1d8e
status: experimental
description: Detects DNS resolution or network connection to datingprudethimble.com, the credential-stealing/malware domain amplified via X's Grok chatbot in the October 2025 indirect prompt-injection malvertising campaign.
references:
  - https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/social-media/ai-aided-malvertising-chatbot-scams/
  - https://x.com/bananahacks/status/1963184353250353488
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/21

  - attack.t1566.002
  - attack.t1204.001
logsource:
  category: dns
detection:
  selection:
    QueryName|contains: 'datingprudethimble.com'
  condition: selection
falsepositives:
  - Security researcher or sandbox manually detonating the IOC
  - Threat-intel enrichment pipeline resolving the domain
level: high
(source:windows.sysmon AND ((@EventID:22 AND @QueryName:(*datingprudethimble.com* OR *DATINGPRUDETHIMBLE.COM*)) OR (@EventID:3 AND @DestinationHostname:(*datingprudethimble.com* OR *DATINGPRUDETHIMBLE.COM*)))) OR (source:windows.defender AND @ThreatName:*datingprudethimble*) OR (@network.destination.domain:*datingprudethimble.com* OR @http.url:*datingprudethimble.com* OR @url.domain:*datingprudethimble.com*)
(#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/datingprudethimble\.com/i) OR (#event_simpleName=NetworkConnectIP4 ContextImageFileName=/(chrome|msedge|firefox|brave|opera|iexplore)\.exe$/i) AND regex("datingprudethimble\\.com", field=DomainName)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, DomainName, ContextImageFileName, ContextProcessId], function=[count(), min(@timestamp), max(@timestamp)])

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count
    from datamodel=Email.All_Email
    where All_Email.file_name!="-"
    by All_Email.src_user, All_Email.recipient, All_Email.file_name, All_Email.subject
| rename All_Email.recipient as user
| join type=inner user
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
        where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
          AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]
| tstats summariesonly=false count
    from datamodel=Email.All_Email
    where All_Email.file_name!="-"
    by All_Email.src_user, All_Email.recipient, All_Email.file_name, All_Email.subject
| rename All_Email.recipient as user
| join type=inner user
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
        where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
          AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]
Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process Exploitation High
Classic macro/exploit pattern — Office app launching cmd/powershell/wmic/regsvr32.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1059.005 T1218
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","onenote.exe","mspub.exe","visio.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\winword.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\excel.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\powerpnt.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\outlook.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\onenote.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\mspub.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\visio.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Office Application Spawning Script Host
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d07
status: experimental
description: |
    Word/Excel/Outlook/OneNote spawning powershell, cmd, wscript,
    cscript, mshta, rundll32, regsvr32 — classic macro / embedded-
    object payload signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059
    - attack.t1566.001
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\winword.exe'
            - '\excel.exe'
            - '\powerpnt.exe'
            - '\outlook.exe'
            - '\onenote.exe'
            - '\mspub.exe'
            - '\visio.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","onenote.exe","mspub.exe","visio.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","onenote.exe","mspub.exe","visio.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) Exploitation High
Browser-pasted PowerShell launched from explorer/Run dialog — ClickFix social engineering.
ATT&CKT1204.004 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\explorer.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\RuntimeBroker.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web Network_Resolution.DNS DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("") or RemoteUrl has_any ("datingprudethimble.com")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic([""]);
let BadDomains = dynamic(["datingprudethimble.com"]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Outbound Connection to Article-Named IP / Domain
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0f

description: |

    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.dest IN ("")
    by All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest, All_Traffic.dest_port
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`

Critical

Phishing Campaign Leveraging the NPM Ecosystem

Snyk
2025-10-09 12 use cases 4 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A new phishing campaign weaponizes NPM and the unpkg CDN. Over 175 throwaway packages are used to host scripts that redirect users to credential-harvesting sites. The attack targets enterprise employees through the browser, not developers at install time.

ATT&CKT1027 T1190 T1195.002 T1566
Domainscfn.jackpotmastersdanske.com musicboxcr.com villasmbuva.co.mz cfn.fejyhy.com cfn.fenamu.com cfn.notwinningbutpartici.com elkendinsc.com

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Inbound email with HTML attachment linking to unpkg.com Beamglea package · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Browser load of Beamglea redirect-* or mad-* package script from unpkg.com · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Phishing Campaign Leveraging the NPM Ecosystem
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Beamglea mad-* dead-drop fetch from raw.githubusercontent.com/Abassdos2992 · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Connection to Beamglea phishing credential-harvesting domains
[LLM] Inbound email with HTML attachment linking to unpkg.com Beamglea package Delivery High
Detects emails delivering HTML lure files (often disguised as invoices) that embed a <script src> reference to unpkg.com pointing at Beamglea redirect-* or mad-* packages. This is the initial-access vector specific to the October 2025 npm CDN phishing campaign described by Socket and Snyk. Rationale: Anchored on the article-specific package-name regex (redirect-[a-z0-9]{6} and the mad-x.x.x.x.x.x scheme) and the beamglea.js script-filename string verbatim from the Socket/Snyk write-up. The join across EmailAttachmentInfo+EmailUrlInfo on NetworkMessageId is the canonical Defender pattern for 'HTML attachment containing weaponised URL'. Sentinel sentinel_kql degraded to attachment-metadata only because OfficeActivity does not expose URLs inside attachments. Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/beamglea-phishing-campaign
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1102.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email
let lookback = 7d;
let htmlAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
    | where FileType in~ ("html","htm") or FileName endswith ".html" or FileName endswith ".htm";
let unpkgUrls = EmailUrlInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
    | where Url has "unpkg.com"
    | where Url matches regex @"(?i)unpkg\.com/(redirect-[a-z0-9]{6}|mad-[0-9]+(?:\.[0-9]+){5,})(@|/)"
        or Url has_any ("/beamglea.js","beamglea");
htmlAttachments
| join kind=inner unpkgUrls on NetworkMessageId
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, FileName, FileType, SHA256, Url, UrlDomain
// Sentinel cannot inspect URLs embedded inside HTML attachments natively; OfficeActivity surfaces the attachment metadata only.
OfficeActivity
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RecordType in (28, 2)
| where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive","Create")
| where tostring(ItemAttachments) has_any (".html",".htm")
| project TimeGenerated, UserId, Operation, Subject = tostring(parse_json(tostring(Parameters))), Sender = tostring(UserId), Recipients = tostring(parse_json(tostring(Parameters))), ItemAttachments, ClientIP

[LLM] Browser load of Beamglea redirect-* or mad-* package script from unpkg.com Exploitation High
Detects a browser process fetching a JavaScript asset from unpkg.com under a Beamglea-style package name (redirect-[a-z0-9]{6} or mad-x.x.x.x.x.x). This is the execution stage where the lure HTML pulls beamglea.js / script.js and triggers the phishing redirect. Rationale: Anchored on two article-verbatim package-naming regexes (Block 3: redirect-[a-z0-9]{6}; Block 2: mad-x.x.x.x.x.x with sample versions mad-1.0.0.2.2.8 .. mad-10.2.1.2.2.8) plus the unpkg.com CDN host (Block 1) and beamglea.js/script.js filenames called out verbatim in the body. Filtering to browser-process initiators eliminates the legitimate developer use of unpkg via curl/npm. Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/beamglea-phishing-campaign

[LLM] Beamglea mad-* dead-drop fetch from raw.githubusercontent.com/Abassdos2992 Command & Control High

Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim GitHub raw-content URL from the malware payload string table ('https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Abassdos2992/truboebvitalya/refs/heads/main/mad4.txt'), specifically the unique attacker repo path Abassdos2992/truboebvitalya and the mad4.txt resolver filename — both are extremely high-fidelity strings that should not appear in legitimate enterprise traffic.

[LLM] Connection to Beamglea phishing credential-harvesting domains Actions on Objectives High
Detects DNS resolution or HTTP connection to any of the seven Beamglea campaign credential-harvesting domains (jackpotmastersdanske.com, musicboxcr.com, villasmbuva.co.mz, fejyhy.com, fenamu.com, notwinningbutpartici.com, elkendinsc.com), which the unpkg-delivered Beamglea script redirects victims to after parsing the email address from the URL fragment. Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim seven-domain IOC list pre-extracted from the Snyk write-up (cfn.jackpotmastersdanske.com, musicboxcr.com, villasmbuva.co.mz, cfn.fejyhy.com, cfn.fenamu.com, cfn.notwinningbutpartici.com, elkendinsc.com). Multi-table union on Sentinel covers proxy (ImWebSession) and DNS (ImDnsActivity) telemetry because tenants vary in which they ship; both use the correct schema columns (DvcHostname / SrcHostname, never DeviceName). Cross-checked against: • https://socket.dev/blog/beamglea-phishing-campaign
